### §9.4 Elliptic Regularity and the Elliptic Estimate
- `Thm - Elliptic Estimate for Constant-Coefficient Operators on the Torus` — type: theorem; source items: (the first step of the route the orchestrator prescribed; feeds A-T5.2.1); prereqs: [Def - Fourier Series on the Torus and Sobolev Spaces of Integer Order, Thm - Sobolev Norms on the Torus via Fourier Coefficients, Def - Elliptic Differential Operator and Principal Symbol]; statement: Let $P = \sum_{|\alpha|=\ell}A_\alpha\partial^\alpha$ with constant matrices $A_\alpha\in\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb C^r,\mathbb C^r)$ and elliptic symbol $\sigma(\xi) = \sum_{|\alpha|=\ell}A_\alpha\xi^\alpha$ (homogeneous), and set $c := \min_{|\xi|=1}\|\sigma(\xi)^{-1}\|^{-1}>0$. Then for every $k\in\mathbb Z$ and $u\in H_{k+\ell}(T^n;\mathbb C^r)$: $\|u\|_{k+\ell}\le C(\|Pu\|_k + \|u\|_k)$ with $C$ depending only on $c$, $\ell$, $k$; and if $u\in H_m$ for some $m$ and $Pu\in H_k$ then $u\in H_{k+\ell}$; proof: $\widehat{Pu}(\xi) = \sigma(i\xi)\hat u(\xi) = i^\ell\sigma(\xi)\hat u(\xi)$, so $|\hat u(\xi)|\le c^{-1}|\xi|^{-\ell}|\widehat{Pu}(\xi)|$ for $\xi\ne0$ (homogeneity: $\|\sigma(\xi)^{-1}\|\le c^{-1}|\xi|^{-\ell}$); multiply by $(1+|\xi|^2)^{(k+\ell)/2}$, use $(1+|\xi|^2)^{\ell/2}|\xi|^{-\ell}\le2^{\ell/2}$ for $|\xi|\ge1$ and treat $|\xi|<1$ by $\|u\|_k$; regularity is the same computation read as a statement about the sequence $\hat u$; spec: mechanism: **on the Fourier side an elliptic constant-coefficient operator is multiplication by an invertible matrix growing like $|\xi|^\ell$, so it is an isomorphism $H_{k+\ell}\to H_k$ modulo finitely many frequencies**.
- `Thm - Friedrichs Mollifier Lemma on the Torus` — type: theorem; source items: (the regularity engine; feeds A-T5.2.1, A-I7.1.6); prereqs: [Def - Fourier Series on the Torus and Sobolev Spaces of Integer Order, Thm - Sobolev Norms on the Torus via Fourier Coefficients, Thm - Fourier Series of Smooth Functions on the Torus]; statement: Fix $\chi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n)$ with $\chi = 1$ on $|\xi|\le1$, $0\le\chi\le1$, and for $0<\epsilon\le1$ let $J_\epsilon u := \sum_\xi\chi(\epsilon\xi)\hat u(\xi)e^{i\langle\xi,x\rangle}$. Then: (i) $J_\epsilon:H_k\to C^\infty$ for every $k$, $\|J_\epsilon u\|_k\le\|u\|_k$, and $J_\epsilon u\to u$ in $H_k$ as $\epsilon\to0$ for $u\in H_k$; (ii) $J_\epsilon$ commutes with constant-coefficient operators; (iii) for $a\in C^\infty(T^n)$ and every $t\in\mathbb Z$: $\|[a,J_\epsilon]v\|_{t+1}\le C_{a,t}\|v\|_t$ uniformly in $\epsilon$, and $[a,J_\epsilon]v\to0$ in $H_{t+1}$ as $\epsilon\to0$ for $v\in H_t$; (iv) consequently for a differential operator $P$ of order $\ell$ with smooth coefficients: $\|[P,J_\epsilon]v\|_{t}\le C\|v\|_{t+\ell-1}$ uniformly in $\epsilon$ and $[P,J_\epsilon]v\to0$ in $H_t$ for $v\in H_{t+\ell-1}$; (v) (lower semicontinuity) for $u\in H_m$ with $\sup_\epsilon\|J_\epsilon u\|_{s}<\infty$ for some $s>m$: $u\in H_s$ and $\|u\|_s\le\liminf_\epsilon\|J_\epsilon u\|_s$; proof: (i) weights $\chi(\epsilon\xi)\le1$ and dominated convergence for series (monotone/dominated convergence for sums proved directly: tail estimate); (iii) $\widehat{[a,J_\epsilon]v}(\xi) = \sum_\eta\hat a(\xi-\eta)(\chi(\epsilon\eta)-\chi(\epsilon\xi))\hat v(\eta)$; Lemma 1: $|\chi(\epsilon\eta)-\chi(\epsilon\xi)|(1+|\xi|)\le C(1+|\xi-\eta|)^2$ uniformly in $\epsilon\le1$ (if $\chi(\epsilon\eta)\ne\chi(\epsilon\xi)$ then $\min(|\xi|,|\eta|)\le R/\epsilon$ with $R$ the support radius, and $|\chi(\epsilon\eta)-\chi(\epsilon\xi)|\le\epsilon|\xi-\eta|\sup|\nabla\chi|$ by the mean value theorem — the two cases written out); then Peetre and the rapid decay of $\hat a$ give a convolution bound $\|[a,J_\epsilon]v\|_{t+1}\le C\sum_\zeta|\hat a(\zeta)|(1+|\zeta|)^{2+|t|+1}\cdot\|v\|_t$ via Young's inequality $\ell^1*\ell^2\subset\ell^2$; convergence to zero: true for trigonometric polynomials $v$ (finite sums, each term $\to0$), and the uniform bound plus density gives the general case ($\epsilon/3$); (iv) $P = \sum_\alpha a_\alpha\partial^\alpha$, $[P,J_\epsilon] = \sum_\alpha[a_\alpha,J_\epsilon]\partial^\alpha$ by (ii); (v) $\|u\|_s^2 = \sum(1+|\xi|^2)^s|\hat u|^2 = \lim_{N}\sum_{|\xi|\le N}\dots$ and for each finite set of $\xi$, $\sum_{|\xi|\le N}(1+|\xi|^2)^s|\hat u|^2 = \lim_\epsilon\sum_{|\xi|\le N}\chi(\epsilon\xi)^2(\dots)\le\liminf_\epsilon\|J_\epsilon u\|_s^2$; spec: reference Lawson–Michelsohn III.5 (Friedrichs' lemma) and Wells IV.2 ("Friedrichs mollifiers"); mechanism: **a smooth cutoff in frequency almost commutes with multiplication by a smooth function, with the error gaining one derivative because the cutoff varies slowly on the scale of its support**.
- `Thm - Elliptic Regularity and the Elliptic Estimate` — type: theorem (anchor; two parts); source items: A-T5.2.1 (Theorem 146), A-I5.2.1, A-I7.1.6; prereqs: [Thm - Elliptic Estimate for Constant-Coefficient Operators on the Torus, Thm - Friedrichs Mollifier Lemma on the Torus, Thm - Differential Operators are Bounded between Sobolev Spaces, Thm - Sobolev Spaces of Sections on a Compact Manifold via Charts, Thm - Sobolev Embedding Theorem, Thm - Existence of Smooth Partitions of Unity]; statement: Let $M$ be a compact manifold and $L:\Gamma(E)\to\Gamma(F)$ an elliptic differential operator of order $\ell$. (i) (Elliptic estimate) For every $k\in\mathbb Z$ and every $m<k+\ell$ there is $C = C(L,k,m)$ with $\|u\|_{k+\ell}\le C(\|Lu\|_k + \|u\|_m)$ for all $u\in H_{k+\ell}(M;E)$; in particular, for $k\ge0$, $\|u\|_{W^{k+\ell,2}}\le C(\|Lu\|_{W^{k,2}} + \|u\|_{L^2})$ (Haydys's (146) with $p = 2$). (ii) (Regularity) If $u\in H_m(M;E)$ for some $m\in\mathbb Z$ and $Lu\in H_k(M;F)$ (in the weak sense of the boundedness page), then $u\in H_{k+\ell}(M;E)$ and the estimate (i) holds for it; in particular if $Lu$ is smooth then $u$ is smooth. **Convention callout:** Haydys states (146) for $W^{k,p}$, all $p>1$; the $L^p$ case is Calderón–Zygmund theory (Gilbarg–Trudinger Ch. 9; Taylor *PDE III* Ch. 13) and is not proved or used in the series; proof: **Step 1** (freezing): for $x_0$ in a chart, $P$ the local operator, $P_0$ its top-order part with coefficients frozen at $x_0$; for $u$ supported in the ball $B_\delta(x_0)$: $\|u\|_{k+\ell}\le C_0(\|P_0u\|_k + \|u\|_k)$ (constant-coefficient page) and $\|(P-P_0)u\|_k\le\eta(\delta)\|u\|_{k+\ell} + C_\delta\|u\|_{k+\ell-1}$ where $\eta(\delta)\to0$ as $\delta\to0$ (Lemma 1: the top-order part of $P-P_0$ has coefficients bounded by $\eta(\delta)$ on $B_\delta$; multiplication by a smooth function $b$ supported in $B_{2\delta}$ with $\|b\|_{C^0}\le\eta$ satisfies $\|b\,w\|_k\le\eta\|w\|_k + C\|w\|_{k-1}$ for $k\ge0$ by Leibniz, and for $k<0$ by duality; the lower-order part of $P-P_0$ is bounded $H_{k+\ell-1}\to H_k$); choose $\delta$ with $C_0\eta(\delta)\le\tfrac12$ and absorb: $\|u\|_{k+\ell}\le C(\|Pu\|_k + \|u\|_{k+\ell-1})$ for $u$ supported in $B_\delta(x_0)$. **Step 2** (patching): cover $M$ by finitely many such balls, take a subordinate partition of unity $(\phi_i)$; for $u\in\Gamma(E)$, $\|u\|_{k+\ell}\le C\sum_i\|\phi_iu\|_{k+\ell}\le C\sum_i(\|L(\phi_iu)\|_k + \|\phi_iu\|_{k+\ell-1})\le C(\|Lu\|_k + \|u\|_{k+\ell-1})$ using $L(\phi_iu) = \phi_iLu + [L,\phi_i]u$ with $[L,\phi_i]$ of order $\ell-1$ (Lemma 2, from the symbol page). **Step 3** (interpolation): $\|u\|_{k+\ell-1}\le\epsilon\|u\|_{k+\ell} + C_\epsilon\|u\|_m$ absorbs the middle term, giving (i) for smooth $u$, then for all $u\in H_{k+\ell}$ by density. **Step 4** (regularity): it suffices to show $u\in H_m$, $Lu\in H_k$, $m<k+\ell$ imply $u\in H_{m+1}$ (then iterate); localise: $\phi_iu\in H_m$ with $L(\phi_iu) = \phi_iLu + [L,\phi_i]u\in H_{\min(k,m-\ell+1)}$; transport to the torus and apply the estimate of Step 1–3 (valid on the torus for the localised operator, with the Step-1 constant) to the smooth sections $J_\epsilon(\phi_iu)$: $\|J_\epsilon(\phi_iu)\|_{m+1}\le C(\|PJ_\epsilon(\phi_iu)\|_{m+1-\ell} + \|J_\epsilon(\phi_iu)\|_m)\le C(\|J_\epsilon P(\phi_iu)\|_{m+1-\ell} + \|[P,J_\epsilon](\phi_iu)\|_{m+1-\ell} + \|\phi_iu\|_m)$, and each term is bounded uniformly in $\epsilon$: the first since $P(\phi_iu)\in H_{m+1-\ell}$ (as $m+1-\ell\le k$), the second by Friedrichs (iv) with $t = m+1-\ell$, $t+\ell-1 = m$; hence by Friedrichs (v), $\phi_iu\in H_{m+1}$; sum over $i$ (a technical point: the localised operator $P$ on the torus must be elliptic on all of $T^n$ — extend the coefficients from the chart image to the torus keeping ellipticity, e.g. by interpolating with the frozen operator $P_0$ via a cutoff, which is possible on a small enough ball since ellipticity is an open condition on the symbol — Lemma 3, written out); spec: references Lawson–Michelsohn Thm III.5.2 and the discussion before it; Wells IV Thm 4.? (elliptic regularity via Friedrichs); Taylor *PDE I* §5.11 (interior regularity by difference quotients — alternative recorded); mechanism: **an elliptic operator is invertible at the level of symbols, and the symbol calculus commutes with everything up to lower order; regularity follows because the a priori estimate applied to mollifications is uniform**; What Makes This Hard: the freezing constant must be uniform in the ball radius, and the estimate must be applied to *smooth approximants* of $u$, not to $u$ itself.
- `Ex - The Constant-Coefficient Elliptic Estimate on the Torus` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐; source items: (drill); prereqs: [Thm - Elliptic Estimate for Constant-Coefficient Operators on the Torus]; spec: for $P = \Delta + c$ on $T^n$ compute the best constant in $\|u\|_{k+2}\le C(\|Pu\|_k + \|u\|_k)$ from the Fourier side; then show that for $P = \partial_1^2 - \partial_2^2$ (not elliptic) no such estimate holds (plane waves along the light cone).
- `Ex - Garding's Inequality from the Elliptic Estimate` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐; source items: (the orchestrator's route item; additional); prereqs: [Thm - Elliptic Regularity and the Elliptic Estimate, Thm - Existence and Ellipticity of the Formal Adjoint]; spec: for a first-order elliptic operator $D$ on a compact manifold prove Gårding's inequality $\|Du\|_0^2\ge c\|u\|_1^2 - C\|u\|_0^2$ from the elliptic estimate with $k = 0$, $\ell = 1$; deduce $\langle D^*Du,u\rangle\ge c\|u\|_1^2 - C\|u\|_0^2$; then for a second-order operator $P = D^*D + $ (order $\le1$), $\operatorname{Re}\langle Pu,u\rangle\ge c'\|u\|_1^2 - C'\|u\|_0^2$ (absorb the first-order term by interpolation).
- `Ex - Interpolation Inequality for Sobolev Norms` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐; source items: (drill of part (v) of the torus-norms page); prereqs: [Thm - Sobolev Norms on the Torus via Fourier Coefficients]; spec: prove $\|u\|_t\le\epsilon\|u\|_s + C_\epsilon\|u\|_r$ for $r<t<s$ with an explicit $C_\epsilon$ (Young's inequality with exponents $(s-r)/(t-r)$ and $(s-r)/(s-t)$); show the inequality fails with $\epsilon = 0$.
- `Ex - Weak Solutions of the Laplace Equation on the Torus are Smooth` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐; source items: (the model case of regularity); prereqs: [Thm - Elliptic Estimate for Constant-Coefficient Operators on the Torus, Thm - Sobolev Embedding Theorem]; spec: let $u\in L^2(T^n)$ satisfy $\int u\,\Delta\phi = \int f\phi$ for all smooth $\phi$ with $f\in C^\infty$; prove $u\in\bigcap_kH_k$, hence smooth, using only Fourier coefficients (no mollifiers needed since the coefficients are constant); identify the obstruction $\xi = 0$ (the compatibility condition $\hat f(0) = 0$ and the one-dimensional kernel).
- `Exercise Index - §9.4 Elliptic Regularity and the Elliptic Estimate` — lists: [Ex - The Constant-Coefficient Elliptic Estimate on the Torus, Ex - Garding's Inequality from the Elliptic Estimate, Ex - Interpolation Inequality for Sobolev Norms, Ex - Weak Solutions of the Laplace Equation on the Torus are Smooth]

### §9.5 Fredholm Operators and the Fredholm Property of Elliptic Operators
- `Def - Fredholm Operator and Index` — type: definition (anchor); source items: A-D5.2.6 (Definition 150), A-E5.2.4 (→ inside, Examples: a linear map between finite-dimensional spaces is Fredholm with index $\dim X - \dim Y$; source typo: Haydys p. 48 prints $\dim Y - \dim X$), A-R5.2.4 (→ inside, Axiom Motivation: Fredholm operators are the infinite-dimensional operators that behave like finite-dimensional ones); prereqs: [Def - Cauchy Sequence and Complete Metric Space, Def - Quotient Space, Thm - Fundamental Theorem of Linear Maps]; spec: a bounded linear $B:X\to Y$ between Banach spaces is *Fredholm* if (a) $\dim\ker B<\infty$, (b) $\operatorname{Im}B$ is closed, (c) $\dim\operatorname{coker}B<\infty$ with $\operatorname{coker}B := Y/\operatorname{Im}B$; $\operatorname{index}B := \dim\ker B - \dim\operatorname{coker}B$; (b) is redundant (next page) but kept in the definition as Haydys does; $\operatorname{Fred}(X,Y)$ with the operator-norm topology; examples: finite-dimensional maps (index $\dim X-\dim Y$, verified), the identity (index 0), the unilateral shift on $\ell^2$ (index $-1$; its adjoint index $+1$; verified), $d/d\theta:H_1(S^1)\to H_0(S^1)$ (index $0$, kernel and cokernel the constants — verified by Fourier); non-examples: the inclusion $H_1\subset H_0$ (infinite cokernel), multiplication by $x$ on $L^2[0,1]$ (dense non-closed range); Convention callout: Haydys's cokernel is the algebraic quotient; in Hilbert spaces $\operatorname{coker}B\cong(\operatorname{Im}B)^\perp$ once the range is closed.
- `Thm - Closed Range is Automatic for Finite-Dimensional Cokernel` — type: theorem; source items: A-T5.2.4 (Remark 151), A-I5.2.4; prereqs: [Def - Fredholm Operator and Index, Thm - Banach–Steinhaus and Open Mapping (Application of Baire)]; statement: Let $B:X\to Y$ be a bounded linear map between Banach spaces with $\dim\ker B<\infty$ and $\dim(Y/\operatorname{Im}B)<\infty$ (algebraic quotient). Then $\operatorname{Im}B$ is closed; proof: **decision: the page states and proves the theorem for Hilbert spaces $X,Y$** (all the series uses; in a Banach space one needs the Hahn–Banach theorem to complement $\ker B$, which is not in the vault — the Banach case is recorded as bold context with the reference Brezis, *Functional Analysis*, Prop. 2.16 and Thm 2.19): let $X_1 := (\ker B)^\perp$; then $\tilde B:X_1\oplus V\to Y$, $(x,v)\mapsto Bx + v$, with $V$ a finite-dimensional algebraic complement of $\operatorname{Im}B$, is a bounded bijection, hence an isomorphism by the open mapping theorem (`Thm - Banach–Steinhaus and Open Mapping (Application of Baire)` — the writer checks that page proves the open mapping theorem in full; the grep shows it does), so $\operatorname{Im}B = \tilde B(X_1\oplus0)$ is closed; spec: mechanism: **a bijective bounded map between Banach spaces is a homeomorphism, so the image of a closed subspace is closed**.
- `Thm - Stabilisation of Fredholm Operators and Continuity of the Kernel` — type: theorem (the stabilisation lemma used by the stability theorem here and by the determinant line bundle in X); source items: A-I6.4.1, A-I6.4.2, A-R6.4.2 (the exact sequence (170)); prereqs: [Def - Fredholm Operator and Index, Thm - Closed Range is Automatic for Finite-Dimensional Cokernel, Thm - Banach–Steinhaus and Open Mapping (Application of Baire)]; statement: Let $X,Y$ be Hilbert spaces, $T:X\to Y$ Fredholm, and $V\subset Y$ a finite-dimensional subspace with $\operatorname{Im}T + V = Y$. Define $T_V:X\oplus V\to Y$, $T_V(x,v) := Tx + v$. Then: (i) $T_V$ is surjective and Fredholm with $\dim\ker T_V<\infty$, and there is an exact sequence $0\to\ker T\to\ker T_V\to V\to V/(\operatorname{Im}T\cap V)\to0$ with $\operatorname{coker}T\cong V/(\operatorname{Im}T\cap V)$; consequently $\operatorname{index}T = \dim\ker T_V - \dim V$. (ii) (Openness of surjectivity) If $S:X\oplus V\to Y$ is bounded and surjective with finite-dimensional kernel, then every $S'$ with $\|S'-S\|<\delta(S)$ is surjective with $\dim\ker S' = \dim\ker S$. (iii) (Continuity of the kernel) For a continuous family $p\mapsto S_p\in\operatorname{Hom}(Z,Y)$ ($Z$ Hilbert) of surjective operators with finite-dimensional kernels, near any $p_0$ there is a continuous family of linear isomorphisms $\ker S_{p_0}\to\ker S_p$; hence $\{\ker S_p\}$ is a locally trivial (continuous) vector bundle over the parameter space; proof: (i) exactness by direct verification (the maps: inclusion, $(x,v)\mapsto v$, $v\mapsto[v]$); dimension count; (ii)–(iii): write $Z = \ker S\oplus Z_1$ (orthogonal complement); $S|_{Z_1}:Z_1\to Y$ is a bounded bijection, hence has a bounded inverse $R$ (open mapping); for $\|S'-S\|<\|R\|^{-1}$, $S'|_{Z_1} = S|_{Z_1}(1 + R(S'-S)|_{Z_1})$ is invertible (Neumann series, Lemma 1), so $S'$ is surjective and $\ker S' = \{k - (S'|_{Z_1})^{-1}S'k : k\in\ker S\}$ is the graph of a bounded map $\ker S\to Z_1$ depending continuously on $S'$ — this gives (iii) with the isomorphism $k\mapsto k - (S_p|_{Z_1})^{-1}S_pk$; spec: reference McDuff–Salamon *J-holomorphic Curves* Thm A.2.2 (Haydys's [MS12]) and Lawson–Michelsohn III.§7; mechanism: **adding a finite-dimensional space makes a Fredholm operator surjective, and surjectivity with finite-dimensional kernel is an open condition whose kernel varies as a graph**.
- `Thm - Riesz-Schauder Theory for Compact Perturbations of the Identity` — type: theorem; source items: (foundation for the compact-perturbation part of A-X5.2.1 / A-I7.1.10 and for A-T7.1.20; no numbered item); prereqs: [Def - Compact Operator, Thm - Basic Properties of Compact Operators, Def - Fredholm Operator and Index, Thm - Orthogonal Decomposition]; statement: Let $X$ be a Hilbert space and $K:X\to X$ compact. Then: (i) (Riesz's lemma) a normed space whose closed unit ball is compact is finite-dimensional; (ii) $\ker(1+K)$ is finite-dimensional; (iii) $\operatorname{Im}(1+K)$ is closed; (iv) $\operatorname{Im}(1+K)^\perp = \ker(1+K^*)$ is finite-dimensional; hence $1+K$ is Fredholm; (v) $\operatorname{index}(1+K) = 0$; proof: (i) the standard almost-orthogonality argument (or in Hilbert spaces: an orthonormal sequence has no convergent subsequence); (ii) on $\ker(1+K)$, $K = -1$, so the unit ball is compact; (iii) if $(1+K)x_j\to y$, replace $x_j$ by its component orthogonal to $\ker(1+K)$; if $\|x_j\|$ were unbounded, normalise and extract a convergent subsequence of $Kx_j/\|x_j\|$, giving $x_j/\|x_j\|\to z$ with $(1+K)z = 0$ and $z\perp\ker(1+K)$, $\|z\| = 1$ — contradiction; so $x_j$ bounded, $Kx_j$ has a convergent subsequence, hence $x_j = (1+K)x_j - Kx_j$ converges to some $x$ with $(1+K)x = y$; (iv) $K^*$ compact by the basic-properties page, apply (ii); (v) via finite-rank approximation: $K = K_0 + K_1$ with $K_0$ finite rank and $\|K_1\|<1$; $1+K_1$ invertible (Neumann), so $1+K = (1+K_1)(1+(1+K_1)^{-1}K_0)$ and it suffices to treat $1+F$ with $F$ finite rank: both kernel and cokernel are computed inside $\operatorname{Im}F + $ (a finite-dimensional space containing it), where the index is $0$ by the finite-dimensional formula — the writer does the bookkeeping (Brezis Thm 6.6); spec: reference Brezis *Functional Analysis* Thm 6.6 (Fredholm alternative); mechanism: **a compact operator is small on all but finitely many dimensions, so $1+K$ is invertible up to a finite-dimensional defect of dimension zero**.
- `Thm - Stability of the Fredholm Property under Small and Compact Perturbations` — type: theorem (three parts); source items: A-X5.2.1 (Exercise 152: the small-perturbation statement is its content), A-I7.1.10, A-R7.1.11; prereqs: [Thm - Stabilisation of Fredholm Operators and Continuity of the Kernel, Thm - Riesz-Schauder Theory for Compact Perturbations of the Identity, Thm - Closed Range is Automatic for Finite-Dimensional Cokernel, Def - Compact Operator]; statement: Let $X,Y$ be Hilbert spaces. (i) $\operatorname{Fred}(X,Y)$ is open in the space of bounded operators, and $\operatorname{index}:\operatorname{Fred}(X,Y)\to\mathbb Z$ is locally constant; in particular for a continuous path $T_t$ of Fredholm operators, $\operatorname{index}T_0 = \operatorname{index}T_1$. (ii) If $T$ is Fredholm and $K$ is compact then $T+K$ is Fredholm and $\operatorname{index}(T+K) = \operatorname{index}T$. (iii) (Atkinson) $T$ is Fredholm if and only if there is a bounded $S:Y\to X$ with $ST - 1$ and $TS - 1$ compact; proof: (i) choose $V$ with $\operatorname{Im}T + V = Y$; for $T'$ near $T$, $T'_V$ is surjective with $\dim\ker T'_V = \dim\ker T_V$ (stabilisation (ii)); then $\ker T'\subset\ker T'_V$ is finite-dimensional, $\operatorname{coker}T'$ is a quotient of $V$ (since $\operatorname{Im}T' + V = Y$), so $T'$ is Fredholm (closed range automatic) with $\operatorname{index}T' = \dim\ker T'_V - \dim V = \operatorname{index}T$; (iii) "$\Rightarrow$": $S := $ (inverse of $T$ on $(\ker T)^\perp\to\operatorname{Im}T$) composed with the orthogonal projection onto $\operatorname{Im}T$; then $ST = 1 - P_{\ker T}$, $TS = 1 - P_{(\operatorname{Im}T)^\perp}$, finite rank; "$\Leftarrow$": $\ker T\subset\ker(ST) = \ker(1+K_1)$ finite-dimensional by Riesz–Schauder; $\operatorname{Im}T\supset\operatorname{Im}(TS) = \operatorname{Im}(1+K_2)$ closed of finite codimension, so $\operatorname{coker}T$ is finite-dimensional; (ii) $S(T+K) = 1 + (ST-1) + SK$ and $(T+K)S = 1 + (TS-1) + KS$ are compact perturbations of the identity, so $T+K$ is Fredholm by (iii); the path $T+tK$, $t\in[0,1]$, consists of Fredholm operators, so the index is constant by (i); spec: Haydys leaves (i) as Exercise 152 and uses (ii) silently in the dimension formula (R7.1.11: "a one-parameter family $T_t$ of Fredholm operators has $\operatorname{index}T_0 = \operatorname{index}T_1$"); mechanism: **the Fredholm property and the index are determined by finitely many dimensions that cannot jump under small or compact changes**; targets: the index computation for the Seiberg–Witten operator (XI), determinant line bundles (X).
- `Thm - Elliptic Operators on Closed Manifolds are Fredholm` — type: theorem (anchor); source items: A-T5.2.5 (Theorem 153), A-I5.2.5, A-I7.1.6 (the Fredholm property of $\slashed D^\pm_A$ and $d^++d^*$ as instances, Corollary 1); prereqs: [Thm - Elliptic Regularity and the Elliptic Estimate, Thm - Rellich Compactness Theorem, Thm - Differential Operators are Bounded between Sobolev Spaces, Thm - Existence and Ellipticity of the Formal Adjoint, Thm - Sobolev Spaces of Sections on a Compact Manifold via Charts, Def - Fredholm Operator and Index, Thm - Sobolev Embedding Theorem]; statement: Let $M$ be a closed manifold and $L:\Gamma(E)\to\Gamma(F)$ an elliptic differential operator of order $\ell\ge1$ (with fibre metrics and a Riemannian metric fixed). For every $k\in\mathbb Z$ the extension $L:H_{k+\ell}(M;E)\to H_k(M;F)$ is Fredholm. Moreover: (i) $\ker L\subset C^\infty(M;E)$ and is independent of $k$; (ii) $\operatorname{Im}L = \{f\in H_k(M;F) : \langle f,v\rangle_{L^2} = 0\ \forall v\in\ker L^*\}$ where $\ker L^*\subset C^\infty(M;F)$ is the (finite-dimensional) kernel of the formal adjoint; hence $\operatorname{coker}L\cong\ker L^*$ canonically; (iii) $\operatorname{index}L = \dim\ker L - \dim\ker L^*$ is independent of $k$; (iv) for $k\ge0$, $H_k(M;F) = \operatorname{Im}L\oplus\ker L^*$, $L^2$-orthogonally, and $\Gamma(F) = L(\Gamma(E))\oplus\ker L^*$; proof: Haydys's sketch made complete: (1) kernel smooth: $Lu = 0\in H_k$ for all $k$ ⇒ $u\in\bigcap H_k = C^\infty$ (regularity + embedding); (2) kernel finite-dimensional: the unit ball of $\ker L$ (in $H_{k+\ell}$) is bounded in $H_{k+\ell+1}$ by the elliptic estimate (with $Lu = 0$), hence relatively compact in $H_{k+\ell}$ by Rellich; a Hilbert space with compact unit ball is finite-dimensional (Riesz); (3) closed range: on $(\ker L)^\perp\subset H_{k+\ell}$ the estimate improves to $\|u\|_{k+\ell}\le C\|Lu\|_k$ (Lemma 1: otherwise $u_j\perp\ker L$ with $\|u_j\|_{k+\ell} = 1$, $Lu_j\to0$; by the estimate $\|u_j\|_{k+\ell}\le C(\|Lu_j\|_k + \|u_j\|_{k+\ell-1})$ and Rellich a subsequence converges in $H_{k+\ell-1}$, hence is Cauchy in $H_{k+\ell}$, converging to $u\perp\ker L$, $\|u\| = 1$, $Lu = 0$ — contradiction); a bounded-below operator has closed range (Lemma 2); (4) cokernel: by the duality (iv) of the charts page, $(H_k(F))^*\cong H_{-k}(F)$ via the $L^2$ pairing, and the annihilator of $\operatorname{Im}L$ is $\{v\in H_{-k} : \langle Lu,v\rangle = 0\ \forall u\} = \{v : L^*v = 0 \text{ weakly}\}$ (boundedness page); by regularity for the elliptic operator $L^*$, such $v$ are smooth, so the annihilator is $\ker L^*$ (independent of $k$), finite-dimensional by (2) applied to $L^*$; since $\operatorname{Im}L$ is closed, $\operatorname{coker}L\cong(\operatorname{Im}L)^{\text{ann}}{}^* \cong\ker L^*$ (Lemma 3: for a closed subspace $W$ of a Hilbert space $H$, $H/W\cong W^\perp$ and $W = (W^\perp)^\perp$; here $W^\perp$ is computed with the $L^2$ pairing into $H_{-k}$ — the writer keeps the two inner products apart); (ii)–(iv) follow, the smooth decomposition by regularity; spec: the gaps listed in the content map (closedness of range, regularity of the complement, complementation in $W^{k}$ rather than $L^2$) are exactly Lemmas 1–3; mechanism: **the elliptic estimate plus Rellich makes the kernel finite and the range closed; duality plus regularity for the adjoint makes the cokernel the smooth kernel of $L^*$**.
- `Thm - Fredholm Alternative for Elliptic Operators` — type: theorem (two parts); source items: A-T5.2.2 (Theorem 148; source typo: Haydys prints "$t\in\ker L^*$" for "$t\perp\ker L^*$"), A-T5.2.3 (Remark 149, with the caveat that the dichotomy as printed presupposes $\dim\ker L = \dim\ker L^*$, e.g. index zero — the corrected statement below), A-I5.2.3; prereqs: [Thm - Elliptic Operators on Closed Manifolds are Fredholm]; statement: Let $L$ be elliptic on a closed manifold and $t\in C^\infty(M;F)$. (i) $Ls = t$ has a smooth solution $s$ if and only if $t\perp\ker L^*$ in $L^2$; the solution is unique modulo $\ker L$, and there is a unique solution $L^2$-orthogonal to $\ker L$, depending linearly and continuously (in every Sobolev norm) on $t$. (ii) If $\operatorname{index}L = 0$ (in particular if $L$ is formally self-adjoint), exactly one of the following holds: $L^*s = 0$ has a non-trivial solution, or $Ls = t$ has a unique smooth solution for every smooth $t$; proof: (i) from (iv) of the previous page and regularity; the continuous dependence from the bounded-below estimate on $(\ker L)^\perp$; (ii) $\dim\ker L = \dim\ker L^*$ when the index is $0$, so $\ker L^* = 0$ iff $\ker L = 0$ iff $L$ is bijective; spec: mechanism: the Fredholm alternative is the finite-dimensional rank–nullity theorem transplanted by the Fredholm property; targets: the Hodge theorem (§9.6), the Coulomb gauge (XI).
- `Ex - Index of a Finite-Dimensional Linear Map` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐; source items: A-E5.2.4 (drill; the typo B11 is the illegal-but-tempting step); prereqs: [Def - Fredholm Operator and Index]; spec: prove $\operatorname{index}B = \dim X - \dim Y$ for any linear $B:X\to Y$ between finite-dimensional spaces via rank–nullity; deduce that the index of a Fredholm operator on a finite-dimensional space is independent of the operator.
- `Ex - The Shift Operator is Fredholm` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐; source items: (drill); prereqs: [Def - Fredholm Operator and Index]; spec: compute kernel, range, cokernel and index of the unilateral shift $S$ and of $S^*$ on $\ell^2$; show $S^n$ has index $-n$ and that $\operatorname{index}$ is additive for these; show $S + K$ has index $-1$ for every compact $K$ (using the stability theorem).
- `Ex - Fredholm Operators Form an Open Set` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐; source items: (drill of A-X5.2.1, whose proof lives on the stability page); prereqs: [Thm - Stability of the Fredholm Property under Small and Compact Perturbations]; spec: reprove openness and local constancy of the index directly for operators with $\ker T = 0$ (bounded-below plus finite cokernel), then reduce the general case to it by restricting to $(\ker T)^\perp$; compare with the stabilisation proof.
- `Ex - The Index is Additive under Composition` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐⭐; source items: (additional; Brezis Ex. 6.? / Lawson–Michelsohn III.7); prereqs: [Def - Fredholm Operator and Index, Thm - Stabilisation of Fredholm Operators and Continuity of the Kernel]; spec: prove that for Fredholm $T_1:X\to Y$, $T_2:Y\to Z$ the composite is Fredholm with $\operatorname{index}(T_2T_1) = \operatorname{index}T_1 + \operatorname{index}T_2$; route: the six-term exact sequence $0\to\ker T_1\to\ker(T_2T_1)\to\ker T_2\to\operatorname{coker}T_1\to\operatorname{coker}(T_2T_1)\to\operatorname{coker}T_2\to0$ (construct the connecting map and verify exactness), then the alternating-dimension count for exact sequences of finite-dimensional spaces.
- `Ex - Fredholm Alternative for the Laplacian on the Torus` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐; source items: (drill of the alternative); prereqs: [Thm - Fredholm Alternative for Elliptic Operators]; spec: on $T^n$ show $\Delta u = f$ is solvable iff $\int f = 0$, with the solution unique up to constants, by Fourier series; then verify the statement of `Thm - Fredholm Alternative for Elliptic Operators` for $\Delta + c$, $c>0$ (bijective) and for $\Delta - 1$ on $S^1$ (kernel $e^{\pm i\theta}$).
- `Exercise Index - §9.5 Fredholm Operators and the Fredholm Property of Elliptic Operators` — lists: [Ex - Index of a Finite-Dimensional Linear Map, Ex - The Shift Operator is Fredholm, Ex - Fredholm Operators Form an Open Set, Ex - The Index is Additive under Composition, Ex - Fredholm Alternative for the Laplacian on the Torus]

### §9.6 Elliptic Complexes and the Hodge Theorem
- `Def - Elliptic Complex and Its Laplacians` — type: definition (anchor; compound: complex of differential operators and its cohomology; ellipticity via the symbol sequence; the Laplacians; harmonic sections; $b_j(E)$); source items: A-D5.3.1, A-D5.3.2, A-D5.3.3, A-D5.3.4, A-D5.3.5, A-R5.3.1 (→ inside, Axiom Motivation: the gauge-theoretic reading $\mathcal B = \Gamma(E_j)$, $\mathcal G = \Gamma(E_{j-1})$ acting by $s\mapsto s+Lt$, "moduli space" $L^{-1}(0)/\mathcal G = H^j(E)$; footnote 4 on Sobolev completions), A-R5.3.2 (→ inside, Relate: $H^j(E)$ is never compact; the dilation action and $(H^j\setminus0)/\mathbb R_{>0}$ compact), A-R5.3.4 (→ inside, Examples: $b_j(E)$ as the isomorphism class; source typo: Haydys p. 51 "non-positive integer" should read "non-negative"); prereqs: [Def - Elliptic Differential Operator and Principal Symbol, Def - Formal Adjoint of a Differential Operator, Thm - The Principal Symbol is Well-Defined and Multiplicative, Def - Exact Sequence of Groups]; spec: a sequence $0\to\Gamma(E_1)\xrightarrow{L_1}\Gamma(E_2)\to\cdots\xrightarrow{L_{k-1}}\Gamma(E_k)\to0$ of differential operators with $L_j\circ L_{j-1} = 0$; cohomology $H^j(E) := \ker L_j/\operatorname{Im}L_{j-1}$; *elliptic* if the symbol sequence $0\to\pi^*E_1\xrightarrow{\sigma_{L_1}(\xi)}\pi^*E_2\to\cdots\to\pi^*E_k\to0$ is exact for every $\xi\ne0$; a two-term complex is elliptic iff $L$ is elliptic (proved); Laplacians $\Delta_j := L_j^*L_j + L_{j-1}L_{j-1}^*$ (with fibre metrics; all $L_j$ assumed of order 1 from here on, as Haydys does — remark on the general case); harmonic sections $\mathcal H^j(E) := \ker\Delta_j$; $b_j(E) := \dim H^j(E)$; examples: the de Rham complex (§9.6 last page), the Atiyah complex (XI), the two-term complex of an elliptic operator; non-example: $0\to\Omega^0\xrightarrow{d}\Omega^1\to0$ on a surface (symbol sequence not exact at $\Omega^1$).
- `Thm - The Laplacians of an Elliptic Complex are Elliptic` — type: theorem; source items: A-X5.3.1 (Exercise 155 — its content); prereqs: [Def - Elliptic Complex and Its Laplacians, Thm - The Principal Symbol is Well-Defined and Multiplicative, Thm - Existence and Ellipticity of the Formal Adjoint, Thm - Orthogonal Decomposition]; statement: If the complex is elliptic then each $\Delta_j$ is elliptic (of order 2), with the symbol computed as follows (the writer fixes the signs): with Haydys's convention $\sigma_{L^*}(\xi) = (-1)^\ell\sigma_L(\xi)^*$, for $\ell = 1$ one gets $\sigma_{\Delta}(\xi) = -(\sigma_{L_j}^*\sigma_{L_j} + \sigma_{L_{j-1}}\sigma_{L_{j-1}}^*)$, a negative-definite (hence invertible) endomorphism for $\xi\ne0$; proof: finite-dimensional Hodge lemma (Lemma 1: for an exact sequence of finite-dimensional inner product spaces $\cdots\to V_{j-1}\xrightarrow{a}V_j\xrightarrow{b}V_{j+1}\to\cdots$, the map $b^*b + aa^*$ is invertible on $V_j$ — since $\ker(b^*b+aa^*) = \ker b\cap\ker a^* = \operatorname{Im}a\cap(\operatorname{Im}a)^\perp = 0$ by exactness) applied to the symbol sequence at each $\xi\ne0$; spec: mechanism: **exactness of the symbol sequence is the pointwise Hodge decomposition, and the symbol Laplacian is invertible exactly when there are no pointwise harmonic vectors**.
- `Thm - A Short Complex is Elliptic iff its Folded Operator is Elliptic` — type: theorem; source items: A-X5.3.2 (Exercise 159 — its content), A-R6.6.2 (→ inside, Motivation: folding the deformation complex into $D_x = (R_x^*,d_xF_w)$); prereqs: [Def - Elliptic Complex and Its Laplacians, Thm - Existence and Ellipticity of the Formal Adjoint]; statement: The complex $0\to\Gamma(E_1)\xrightarrow{L_1}\Gamma(E_2)\xrightarrow{L_2}\Gamma(E_3)\to0$ (first-order operators, $L_2L_1 = 0$) is elliptic if and only if $(L_2,L_1^*):\Gamma(E_2)\to\Gamma(E_3\oplus E_1)$ is elliptic; proof: for $\xi\ne0$: the symbol of the folded operator is $v\mapsto(\sigma_2v,-\sigma_1^*v)$ (sign from the adjoint); it is injective iff $\ker\sigma_2\cap\ker\sigma_1^* = 0$ iff $\ker\sigma_2\cap(\operatorname{Im}\sigma_1)^\perp = 0$; exactness at $E_2$ ($\ker\sigma_2 = \operatorname{Im}\sigma_1$) gives this; the writer proves both directions: ($\Rightarrow$) exactness at $E_1$ (injective $\sigma_1$), at $E_2$, at $E_3$ (surjective $\sigma_2$) gives $\dim E_2 = \dim E_1 + \dim E_3$ and $\ker\sigma_2\cap\ker\sigma_1^* = 0$, so the folded symbol is an injective map between spaces of equal dimension, hence invertible; ($\Leftarrow$) if the folded symbol is invertible then $\sigma_1^*$ is surjective (so $\sigma_1$ injective), $\sigma_2$ is surjective, and $\ker\sigma_2\cap(\operatorname{Im}\sigma_1)^\perp = 0$ with the dimension count gives $\ker\sigma_2 = \operatorname{Im}\sigma_1$; spec: mechanism: **a three-term complex is elliptic exactly when its "gauge-fixed" operator is elliptic**; targets: the Seiberg–Witten operator $D_{(\psi,A)}$ (XI).
- `Thm - Hodge Theorem for Elliptic Complexes` — type: theorem (anchor; four parts); source items: A-T5.3.1 (Theorem 156), A-T5.3.2 (the decomposition (158)), A-I5.3.1, A-T5.3.3 (gauge fixing, §5.3.1), A-R5.3.3; prereqs: [Def - Elliptic Complex and Its Laplacians, Thm - The Laplacians of an Elliptic Complex are Elliptic, Thm - Elliptic Operators on Closed Manifolds are Fredholm, Thm - Fredholm Alternative for Elliptic Operators, Thm - Existence and Ellipticity of the Formal Adjoint]; statement: Let $0\to\Gamma(E_1)\xrightarrow{L}\cdots\xrightarrow{L}\Gamma(E_k)\to0$ be an elliptic complex of first-order operators on a closed manifold (all bundles Euclidean). (i) Each $\mathcal H^j(E) = \ker\Delta_j$ is finite-dimensional and consists of smooth sections. (ii) $s\in\mathcal H^j(E)$ if and only if $Ls = 0$ and $L^*s = 0$. (iii) (Hodge decomposition) $\Gamma(E_j) = \mathcal H^j(E)\oplus L(\Gamma(E_{j-1}))\oplus L^*(\Gamma(E_{j+1}))$, the three summands being mutually $L^2$-orthogonal; and $\ker L = \mathcal H^j\oplus\operatorname{Im}L$. (iv) The natural map $\mathcal H^j(E)\to H^j(E)$, $s\mapsto[s]$, is an isomorphism; each cohomology class has a unique harmonic representative, and $L^{-1}(0)/\mathcal G\to\mathcal H^j(E)$, $s\mapsto h(s)$, is a linear isomorphism (gauge fixing); proof: (i) from the Fredholm page applied to the elliptic $\Delta_j$ (kernel smooth and finite-dimensional); (ii) $\langle\Delta s,s\rangle_{L^2} = \|Ls\|^2 + \|L^*s\|^2$ (formal adjoints; smooth $s$ on a closed manifold); (iii) $\Delta_j$ is formally self-adjoint, so by the Fredholm alternative $\Gamma(E_j) = \ker\Delta_j\oplus\Delta_j(\Gamma(E_j))$ orthogonally; $\Delta_jt = L(L^*t) + L^*(Lt)$, giving the three-term decomposition; orthogonality of $\operatorname{Im}L$ and $\operatorname{Im}L^*$: $\langle Lt,L^*r\rangle = \langle LLt,r\rangle = 0$ (this is the "moment's thought" of the source); orthogonality of $\mathcal H^j$ to both from (ii); $\ker L\perp\operatorname{Im}L^*$ likewise, so $\ker L = \mathcal H^j\oplus\operatorname{Im}L$; (iv) surjectivity: for $Ls_0 = 0$, decompose $s_0 = h + Lt$ by (iii), so $[s_0] = [h]$; injectivity: if $h = Lt$ is harmonic then $\|Lt\|^2 = \langle L^*Lt,t\rangle = \langle L^*h,t\rangle = 0$ — Haydys's argument with the "unique solution" step replaced by the decomposition (the content-map gap); the gauge-fixing map $h(s) := $ the harmonic component; spec: Haydys cites [Wel80] for (158) — Wells Thm IV.5.2; mechanism: **self-adjoint elliptic ⇒ finite-dimensional kernel and closed range orthogonal to it; $L^2 = 0$ splits the range of the Laplacian into the two images**; Bridges: `Thm - Hodge Decomposition Theorem` (Hodge I) is the de Rham case stated in the vault.
- `Thm - The de Rham Complex is Elliptic and Harmonic Forms Represent de Rham Cohomology` — type: theorem (four parts); source items: A-X5.3.3 (Exercise 160 — its content), A-D5.3.6 (the de Rham complex with $d^*$, the Hodge–de Rham Laplacian, Betti numbers; the $d^*$ formula is `Thm - The Hodge-de Rham Operator is a Dirac Operator`), A-I5.3.2, A-R5.3.5; prereqs: [Thm - Hodge Theorem for Elliptic Complexes, Thm - The Hodge-de Rham Operator is a Dirac Operator, Def - de Rham Cohomology, Thm - Homotopy Invariance of de Rham Cohomology, Def - Hodge Laplacian, Def - Harmonic Form]; statement: Let $M$ be a closed oriented Riemannian $n$-manifold. (i) The de Rham complex $0\to\Omega^0\xrightarrow{d}\Omega^1\to\cdots\to\Omega^n\to0$ is elliptic: its symbol sequence $0\to\Lambda^0\xrightarrow{\xi\wedge}\Lambda^1\xrightarrow{\xi\wedge}\cdots\to\Lambda^n\to0$ is exact for $\xi\ne0$. (ii) Its Laplacian is the Hodge Laplacian $\Delta = dd^*+d^*d$ with $d^* = (-1)^{n(j-1)+1}\star d\star$ on $j$-forms. (iii) $\mathcal H^j(M) := \ker\Delta_j$ is finite-dimensional, $\mathcal H^j\cong H^j_{dR}(M)$, and $\Omega^j = \mathcal H^j\oplus d\Omega^{j-1}\oplus d^*\Omega^{j+1}$ orthogonally. (iv) The Betti numbers $b_j(M) := \dim\mathcal H^j(M) = \dim H^j_{dR}(M)$ are independent of the metric and invariant under diffeomorphism and smooth homotopy equivalence; proof: (i) the Koszul-complex lemma: for $\xi\ne0$ choose $e_1 = \xi/|\xi|$; the operator $\iota_{e_1}$ is a contracting homotopy: $\xi\wedge\iota_{e_1} + \iota_{e_1}(\xi\wedge\cdot) = |\xi|\,\mathrm{id}$ (proved from the derivation property of $\iota$), so the sequence is exact; (ii) from the Dirac-operator page ($D = d+d^*$, $D^2 = dd^*+d^*d$ since $d^2 = 0 = (d^*)^2$); (iii) the Hodge theorem; (iv) metric independence since $H^j_{dR}$ does not involve the metric; diffeomorphism invariance of $H^j_{dR}$ by pullback; homotopy invariance by `Thm - Homotopy Invariance of de Rham Cohomology` (DG X); **the identification with singular Betti numbers (topological invariance in the strong sense) is the de Rham theorem, whose vault page is flagged: stated as bold context, not invoked**; spec: this page proves in full what the vault's `Thm - Harmonic Forms Represent de Rham Cohomology` and `Thm - Hodge Decomposition Theorem` state; mechanism: **the wedge-with-$\xi$ complex is exact because contraction with $\xi$ is a chain homotopy to a multiple of the identity**; Bridges: $b_2^\pm$ and the intersection form (XIII), $\ker(d^++d^*)$ in XI.
- `Ex - Ellipticity of the Laplacians of a Complex in the Two-Term Case` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐; source items: (drill of the Laplacian-ellipticity theorem in the simplest case); prereqs: [Thm - The Laplacians of an Elliptic Complex are Elliptic]; spec: for an elliptic $L:\Gamma(E)\to\Gamma(F)$ show $L^*L$ and $LL^*$ are elliptic, compute their symbols, and verify the sign convention; check on $L = d + d^*$ and on $L = \bar\partial$ on the torus $T^2 = \mathbb C/\Lambda$.
- `Ex - The Folded Operator of the Atiyah Complex` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐; source items: (drill of the folding theorem toward XI); prereqs: [Thm - A Short Complex is Elliptic iff its Folded Operator is Elliptic, Thm - Laplacians and Dirac Operators are Elliptic]; spec: on an oriented Riemannian 4-manifold verify by symbol computation that $(d^+,d^*):\Omega^1\to\Omega^2_+\oplus\Omega^0$ is elliptic, and deduce that $0\to\Omega^0\xrightarrow{d}\Omega^1\xrightarrow{d^+}\Omega^2_+\to0$ is an elliptic complex (this is Haydys's Proposition 194, whose Thm page is in XI; the exercise supplies the symbol route while the Thm page also records the Dirac route).
- `Ex - The de Rham Complex is Elliptic by the Koszul Homotopy` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐; source items: (drill of part (i)); prereqs: [Thm - The de Rham Complex is Elliptic and Harmonic Forms Represent de Rham Cohomology]; spec: prove $\xi\wedge\iota_v + \iota_v(\xi\wedge\cdot) = \xi(v)\,\mathrm{id}$ on $\Lambda^\bullet$ and deduce exactness of the symbol sequence; show the Dolbeault complex on $\mathbb C^n$ is elliptic by the same argument.
- `Ex - Harmonic Forms on the Flat Torus` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐; source items: (additional; the vault's `Ex - Harmonic 1-Forms on the Torus` is the $n = 2$ case); prereqs: [Thm - The de Rham Complex is Elliptic and Harmonic Forms Represent de Rham Cohomology]; spec: on $T^n$ with the flat metric show $\mathcal H^j = $ the forms with constant coefficients (Fourier: $\Delta$ acts on coefficients as $|\xi|^2$), so $b_j(T^n) = \binom nj$; verify orthogonality of the Hodge decomposition on an explicit non-harmonic 1-form.
- `Ex - Betti Numbers are Invariant under Diffeomorphism and Smooth Homotopy Equivalence` — type: exercise; difficulty: ⭐⭐; source items: A-R5.3.5 (drill of part (iv)); prereqs: [Thm - The de Rham Complex is Elliptic and Harmonic Forms Represent de Rham Cohomology, Thm - Homotopy Invariance of de Rham Cohomology]; spec: prove that $\dim\mathcal H^j(M,g)$ does not depend on $g$ and that a diffeomorphism $\phi:M\to N$ induces $\mathcal H^j(N)\cong\mathcal H^j(M)$ (through de Rham cohomology, not through the metric); explain why "topological invariant" in the strong sense needs the de Rham theorem.
- `Exercise Index - §9.6 Elliptic Complexes and the Hodge Theorem` — lists: [Ex - Ellipticity of the Laplacians of a Complex in the Two-Term Case, Ex - The Folded Operator of the Atiyah Complex, Ex - The de Rham Complex is Elliptic by the Koszul Homotopy, Ex - Harmonic Forms on the Flat Torus, Ex - Betti Numbers are Invariant under Diffeomorphism and Smooth Homotopy Equivalence]

Sources: A §6.1 (pp. 52–53), §6.2 (pp. 53–55), §6.3 (pp. 55–56), §6.4 (pp. 56–57), §6.5 (pp. 58–59), §6.6 (pp. 59–60); also A-T7.1.24, A-T7.1.25 (Lemma 219 / Remark 220 on determinant bundles, placed here). No Bär items.
